A still of a recurring character is a character still only when an image carrier reaches the surface: a reference input or a seed image. Without one it is not, whatever seed it was made with, and the gate reports that absence before the run.

## 7. Viewpoint transfer

The generator usually receives concrete camera direction, not the internal viewpoint profile ID. Translate the profile into:

The director package keeps the world's orientations (who faces what, who has their back to whom); the exact text keeps only what the lens sees from the chosen position. The two are written in different words, and the translation from one to the other is a step of its own, done after the camera is placed.

- external or owned camera;
- owner identity when applicable;
- camera position or owner body state;
- focalization;
- point of audition;
- scale, angle, lens, and movement;
- visible limbs and subjects;
- axis, screen direction, and eyeline requirements;
- start and landing anchors.

The internal profile remains useful for validation, continuity, and review.

## 8. The shot-request

A shot-request is production-facing. It may include:

- identity and state hashes;
- shot camera and visibility obligations;
- visible identity details;
- wardrobe, inventory, relationship, and environment projection;
- selected candidate references;
- requested deliverable.

What comes back may include a still prompt, boundary-frame prompt, reference plan, correction payload, or generation package.

Before sending any of it to a target:

1. inspect the generated or selected media;
2. register it in the asset registry;
3. confirm adoption and effective story range;
4. adapt it to the real target operation;
5. preserve exact target field contents separately.

## 9. Explicit deferral

A requirement may be deferred when its route remains concrete.

```text
Requirement: exact line in canonical voice.
Picture pass: stable external reaction close-up with a one-second hold.
Audio path: approved voice generation and lip synchronization after picture acceptance.
Acceptance: the scene remains incomplete until speaker, wording, timing, and mix pass review.
```

Deferral moves a requirement. It does not delete it.

## 10. Result evidence

After a run, preserve:

- exact target surface and operation;
- submitted files and hashes;
- exact field contents;
- settings;
- prompt rewrite returned by the service, if any;
- every output variant;
- direct observations;
- accepted, rejected, or repair decision;
- finishing record;
- extracted accepted endpoint;
- state and asset write-back.

An observation is made at a scale that resolves what it claims. A contact sheet decides which frames to open. It does not support a statement about screen direction, a marking, or a count, because those are gone at that size, and an observation taken from one is recorded as not observed.

A planned result is not run evidence.

## 11. Artifact completion test

A package is ready only when:

- the director package contains the full intent;
- the operation card names real controls and files;
- every critical requirement has a carrier or accepted variation;
- exact field contents are preserved literally;
- target-facing text is self-contained;
- primary and negative fields agree;
- internal IDs are not mistaken for model bindings;
- unsupported details have concrete alternate routes;
- result status is accurate;
- run evidence points to preserved output.

## 12. Character reference sheet, and the order of regeneration

How a reference image is generated belongs to whatever seals the visual-contract-package. This suite owns when it is used.

Order of work:

1. Complete the reference sheet before any line is shot: one character per image, plain background, even
   light, the framings the coverage needs, in the outfit the episode uses. The agent drafts each sheet as an
   `asset` submission with `--purpose sheet-panel` and one subject; it is text only unless an adopted image
   already exists.
2. Register each reference image, and record which of them holds the character's identity role. The author
   adopts one image per character for the role `ID/identity`. The visual block refuses a frame that shows a
   recurring character beside another subject until that character's adopted image is bound to it.
3. Decide each shot's reference allocation before its text is written.
4. On a canonical change, regenerate the sheet from the new identity, then the shots derived from it.

Rules:

- A canonical change is not repaired shot by shot. A shot's two or three reference images are already
  allocated between face, build, clothing, the other character and the room, so changing one shot's selection
  to fix that shot silently removes what another shot relied on.
- Replacing the asset that holds an identity role makes every derived record stale; no stale shot is
  regenerated before the sheet is.
- A shot's reference count is part of its plan. Two characters in one frame divide the same limit, so each
  character is supported by fewer images than a solo frame would give it, and the prompt states what the
  images then cannot.

---

<!-- Source: references/target-adaptation.md -->

# Target Adaptation as Editorial Rewriting

Target adaptation is not keyword substitution. Re-author the submission around the actual evidence, operations, controls, and limitations the selected surface receives.

## 1. Define the irreducible shot

Before selecting an operation, write:

```text
opening image
viewpoint profile and camera relation
dominant visible change
cause
performance detail
contact and prop continuity
landing image
dialogue and sound
must be exact
may vary
```

This is the creative truth that must survive target rewriting even when its carriers change.

The picture that shows it is not part of that truth. Fixed are the opening state, the landing, and the chain between them; the composition, scale, angle, and camera move that present them are chosen from what the surface draws reliably, and they change freely when the surface fails. A composition that comes back wrong twice with the same symptom is evidence about the surface, not a sentence to rewrite a third time: choose another picture of the same beat. A closer scale, a cut where a move was planned, the side of the room a reference already shows, an insert on the hands instead of a wide on the bodies. Holding to a shot the surface cannot draw spends runs on a preference the story never required.

## 2. Select the operation by the hardest evidence

Ask what is least replaceable:

- exact opening geometry;
- exact endpoint;
- recurring identity;
- complex motion timing;
- hand or body contact;
- camera path;
- exact dialogue;
- state transition;
- multi-character blocking.

Possible routes include:

- text generation for unconstrained composition;
- start image for exact opening geometry;
- first and last frames for exact endpoints;
- subject references for appearance;
- source edit or restyle for timing and camera;
- extension for continuation from accepted output;
- performance transfer for body, face, gesture, or speech;
- conventional edit and post when one pass is not appropriate.

Choose the operation that carries the hardest evidence, then route remaining requirements to compatible media, text, another pass, neighboring shots, or post-production.

## 3. Rewrite according to submitted evidence

The same scene needs different exact text when evidence changes.

### Text-only generation

The prompt establishes camera, scene, character placement, current state, action, physical response, and landing.

### Complete start image

The image carries opening composition, visible identity, state, and camera. Text concentrates on action, performance, physical response, and landing.

### Start and end frames

The images carry both endpoints. Text directs the physically plausible path and preserves state, contact, and prop ownership.

### Character references without a start image

References carry visible appearance. Text must still establish external camera, scale, blocking, environment, contact, action, and landing.

### Source video edit or restyle

Source carries timing, camera, motion, and existing contact. Text states what remains and what changes.

### Extension

Accepted source carries the real opening. Text begins from actual camera, pose, state, prop, and audio tail.

These are different submissions because the target receives different evidence, not because the suite changes an abstract mode value.

## 4. Translate viewpoint into concrete target direction

The target may not understand a viewpoint profile label. Translate it into:

- camera ownership;
- camera position or owner body state;
- focalization;
- point of audition;
- shot scale, angle, lens, and movement;
- visible limbs, subjects, and identity details;
- screen direction, axis, and eyeline;
- start and landing anchors;
- viewpoint transition bridge when applicable.

Keep the internal profile in production records for validation and review.

## 5. Prompt rewriting and hidden transformation

When a service rewrites prompts:

- preserve exact submitted text;
- preserve returned rewritten text when available;
- compare outcomes across controlled submissions;
- move fragile identity, state, and geometry into submitted media;
- avoid attributing behavior to wording alone when the rewritten prompt is unknown;
- record the effect as dated target evidence.

Prompt rewriting changes the evidence available for diagnosis. It does not justify deleting detailed direction from the director package.

## 6. Negative and exclusion fields

Use a separate field only when the exact surface exposes and documents it. Primary text and submitted media still construct desired camera, state, action, contact, and landing.

Before submission:

- compare primary and negative fields together;
- remove stale exclusions left by earlier revisions;
- keep concise unwanted artifact categories in the negative field;
- keep constructive desired behavior in the primary field and media;
- record the exact field or request key.

A negative field is an additional channel, not the whole design.

## 7. Viewpoint-specific fallback

When a target cannot generate a planned multi-viewpoint sequence in one pass:

1. split shots by viewpoint;
2. generate or author boundary media for each shot;
3. use source edit for difficult camera motion;
4. bridge with action, eyeline, object, sound, occlusion, or light;
5. conform and finish the cut in post;
6. preserve one shared state and continuity ledger across all operations.

Do not force all coverage into one prompt when the target has no mechanism for controlled internal cuts.

## 8. State-specific fallback

When the target loses a state feature:

- confirm selected references visibly support it;
- confirm the shot can see it;
- move it into a start or end image when visually critical;
- choose a better angle-matched reference;
- simplify competing action;
- use edit or identity correction;
- route a small surface repair to post;
- accept variation only when canon and user approval allow it.

Record whether the failure belonged to candidate media, identity or state contract, reference selection, shot geometry, target behavior, or finishing.

## 9. Adaptation trace

For every final submission, record where each requirement went:

```text
retained in primary text
carried by submitted media
moved to auxiliary field
moved to another generation or edit
moved to post-production
carried by neighboring shot
accepted as variation
unresolved
```

This trace prevents rich direction from disappearing during concise target rewriting.

## 10. Observable distinction test

A target distinction belongs in the workflow only when it changes at least one concrete artifact:

- created or submitted media;
- target control or request key;
- exact text;
- settings;
- clip or shot split;
- post-production route;
- review evidence;
- fallback.

If two named strategies produce the same files, controls, text, and review, merge the labels while preserving the useful directing knowledge.

## 11. Target adaptation completion test

- the irreducible shot is clear;
- the chosen operation carries the hardest evidence;
- input combinations are documented or directly observed;
- all files map to real controls;
- exact text reflects what media does and does not supply;
- viewpoint grammar is translated into concrete camera direction;
- identity and state have carriers;
- primary and negative fields agree;
- unsupported requirements have alternate routes;
- the adaptation trace is complete;
- run status remains truthful.

---

<!-- Source: references/contact-scenes.md -->

# Physical Contact Across Viewpoints

Contact requires clear ownership, timing, occlusion, support, feedback, state, and landing. Viewpoint changes visibility, not the physical event.

## 1. Universal contact record

For important contact, record:

```text
initiating subject
moving limb or body region
target subject or object
side and approach path
contact point
occlusion order
support and balance
pressure, weight, temperature, and material response
emotional response
release or held end state
prop ownership before and after
```

Use this record across every viewpoint.

## 2. Viewpoint-specific staging

### External camera

Choose an angle that keeps ownership and support readable. Use close coverage only for a distinct contact detail. Match action and ownership across cuts.

### Over-the-shoulder

Keep foreground anatomy attached. Preserve facing-subject hands, expression, and contact without merging them into foreground forms.

### Embodied first person

Assign camera-owner limbs a destination and frame edge. State any contact occlusion, body-caused camera response, and final visibility.

### Device or fixed observer

Respect device position, field of view, mount, and occlusion. Internal records do not make hidden contact visible.

## 3. Handoffs and transfers

A visible transfer has ordered phases:

1. current owner presents the object;
2. receiving hand, paw, manipulator, or other approved anatomy approaches;
3. both share contact briefly;
4. receiving grip closes;
5. old owner releases;
6. object settles with the new owner.

Record object orientation, condition, and support. Canonize transfer only after acceptance. Offscreen transfer needs an approved atomic event and later visible ownership.

## 4. Hugs and body enclosure

Choose the contact type:

- frontal hug;
- side hug;
- over-the-shoulder hug;
- seated embrace;
- protective enclosure;
- brief greeting;
- restrained or reluctant contact.

Record arm ownership, height relation, head position, torso contact, pressure, cloth or fur response, and release. One participant may initially stiffen and then soften. Show the change through breath, shoulders, hands, ears, tail, wings, posture, or weight.

External cameras show both bodies; shoulder and first-person views emphasize response, pressure, occlusion, breath, hands, or balance.

## 5. Face and near-face contact

Lower-complexity options include:

- forehead contact;
- nose-tip contact;
- cheek contact;
- crown or hair contact;
- hand to cheek;
- brief muzzle or beak-side nuzzle;
- hand kiss.

Use approved anatomy. Record approach, point, duration, occlusion, pressure, and retreat. Protect sensitive anatomy, accessories, and camera position.

Prop-mediated near-face contact follows the same rules.

## 6. Carrying, lifting, and support

### One character carries another

Record anatomy-appropriate support. Separate grip establishment from lift, then show weight transfer, material response, and stable hold.

### Object lift

Record grip, mass, center of gravity, limb chain, release, path, and landing. Heavy props affect posture, contact, and pace.

### First-person lift

Name support points and let camera height follow the body. Keep one continuous embodied viewpoint.

## 7. Medical and care contact

Care scenes may include bandaging, inspection, support, feeding, washing, grooming, temperature checking, repair, or assisted movement. Record:

- consent or established relationship boundary when relevant;
- exposed body or material region;
- tool, medicine, cloth, or repair material;
- hand or limb ownership;
- pressure and sensitivity;
- state change;
- privacy and focalization;
- final wardrobe, bandage, equipment, or repaired state.

Do not reveal a concealed injury through an external angle unless the scene disclosure plan allows it.

## 8. Combat and forced contact

Combat requires a clear cause, action line, impact point, reaction, and recovery. Record:

- attacker and defender;
- active limb, weapon, or object;
- trajectory;
- block, dodge, restraint, or impact;
- contact ownership;
- body, clothing, and equipment response;
- ground or environment feedback;
- injury proposal;
- end positions and screen direction.

Do not combine unrelated attacks and camera moves. Injury remains proposed until accepted as a state event.

## 9. State, inventory, and canon

Contact may change:

- prop ownership or possession;
- injury or healing state;
- clothing condition or layer state;
- equipment attachment;
- relationship state;
- knowledge and disclosure;
- character position and support.

Keep planned events separate from contact direction. Canonize only after acceptance; multi-entity ownership changes remain atomic.

## 10. Contact risk and camera choice

On current video surfaces, natural single-body motion (talking, walking, gesturing) holds, while two-body physical interaction such as a handshake, hand-to-hand handoffs, and any shot that keeps hands large in frame for long are where consistency breaks. Keep such contact short, isolate it in its own clip, cover the hands at a scale where errors are small, or carry the contact in an accepted still and cut around it.

Lower geometric complexity:

- simple handoff;
- high-five;
- one-hand support;
- forehead or nose contact;
- side hug;
- stable seated touch.

Moderate complexity:

- carrying;
- close two-hand care;
- over-the-shoulder hug;
- shared tool use;
- face contact with partial occlusion.

Higher complexity:

- spinning while holding;
- walking in a tight embrace;
- several simultaneous contacts;
- intricate interlocked fingers;
- close facial collision during fast camera movement;
- multi-character pile or grapple.

These are editorial starting points, not universal model limits. Update target-scoped tactics only from preserved run evidence.

## 11. Contact continuity review

- limb, hand, paw, wing, or manipulator ownership stays stable;
- digit count and orientation remain readable where required;
- object ownership changes only at the intended phase;
- contact produces pressure, deformation, balance, movement, or sound;
- support and center of gravity remain plausible;
- occlusion is purposeful and viewpoint-consistent;
- state, wardrobe, and equipment respond correctly;
- reaction follows cause;
- the end pose is stable;
- subsequent shots use the accepted contact outcome.

---

<!-- Source: references/performance-details.md -->

# Performance Detail Library

**Preparation and reuse:** Full applicable sources govern initial characterization and a changed scope. While preparing a scene, save actual definitions and applications using [Scene Persona](#scene-persona-material-prepare-once-reuse-within-scope). During writing or resumption in the reviewed scope, verify complete-source commitments and use that document; the instruction to read the full Persona applies to preparation, not to repeated execution within the reviewed scope. Reopen for new participants, topics, knowledge or portrayal intent, even when source hashes are unchanged.


Performance details serve the intended effect: they may change a reading, sustain it, conceal it, or leave an interval deliberately unresolved. Choose strength and duration from the full persona, applicable form, situation, counterpart, style, and viewing conditions, not from an emotion label or a universal restraint rule. See [Production Direction](#purpose-portrayal-and-production-choices) for the choice and observation record.

## 1. Four performance states

Keep these channels separate:

```text
felt state
expressed state
masked or performed state
physiological state
```

A character may feel fear, express calm, maintain a professional mask, and show shallow breathing.

## 2. Observable performance channels and ownership

Potential channels include:

- face, eyes, brows, mouth, muzzle, beak, or optic system;
- head timing and gaze;
- shoulders, spine, pelvis, stance, and weight;
- hands, paws, wings, tail, ears, crest, horns, or other approved anatomy;
- breath, voice, throat, and pacing;
- clothing, carried objects, and material response;
- light and environment response;
- distance, orientation, and contact behavior.

Use only approved anatomy and behavior.

Body-plan channels may include:

- humanlike: jaw, throat, hands, shoulders, posture, gaze, breath;
- long-muzzle canine: ears, muzzle, tail base and tip, jaw, nose contact;
- broad-muzzle feline: eyes, slow blink, whisker region, tail tip, shoulder roll;
- avian: crest, ruff, wing edge, beak orientation, feather compression;
- scaled or draconic: throat, wing membrane, tail, scale sheen, breath;
- equine or large herbivore: ears, neck, hoof, flank tension, head height;
- robot or machine: optic aperture, faceplate angle, joint speed, panel alignment, emissive response.

Character profiles define what is canonical.

## 3. Expression tiers

When amplitude is a meaningful choice, compare suitable forms of an approved signal. The examples below are a vocabulary, not three mandatory alternatives or a fixed body inventory.

### Small

- eye, brow, ear, crest, or optic adjustment;
- breath change;
- grip change;
- weight shift;
- small tail, wing, hand, or panel response.

### Medium

- posture change;
- one step;
- visible withdrawal or approach;
- moderate tail, wing, ruff, shoulder, or joint response;
- object placed down, raised, or repositioned.

### Large

- full-body release;
- defensive expansion;
- jump, charge, collapse, or transformation;
- major contact or relocation;
- approved signature-scale motion.

Select the strength that serves this depiction and remains readable in its context. Restraint, masking, exaggeration, and coordinated large actions are valid choices. Record the purpose of overlap rather than prohibiting it.

## 4. Performance under viewpoint and focalization

State projects differently by viewpoint.

### External character-aligned third person

- full posture, face, and interaction with space;
- social mask and private leakage;
- how other characters respond;
- visible state effects across the whole body.

### External objective third person

- visible behavior and objective sound only;
- no privileged internal interpretation;
- geography, action, and contact receive priority.

### Over-the-shoulder

- foreground tension in shoulder, hand, head, or held object;
- facing subject's expression and reaction;
- shared object, contact, and eyeline relationship.

### Embodied first person

- breath, balance, visible hands or limbs, gaze delay, sound, and contact;
- the camera owner's face remains unavailable unless reflected;
- internal state uses body and audition evidence the viewpoint can carry.

### Device or fixed observer

- performance remains visible only through the device's position, framing, audio path, and technical limitations;
- do not grant the device privileged access to hidden state.

## 5. Stillness

Stillness may communicate authority, grief, embarrassment, attention, tenderness, or danger. When the intended hold needs a visible or audible cue, consider the following examples. Absolute visual stillness or silence may instead be the selected effect:

- breath;
- cloth, hair, fur, feather, or panel movement;
- practical light change;
- weather or environment motion;
- another character's reaction;
- grip pressure;
- small eye, ear, tail, or optic movement;
- ambient sound with a visible cause.

Do not replace meaningful stillness with arbitrary camera motion.

## 6. Idle pose, signature move, and recurring behavior

A recurring character may have one approved idle pose that makes entrances or held shots recognizable and one signature move used sparingly enough to retain meaning.

Record:

```text
trigger
small, medium, or large expression tier
body support and weight
active anatomy
path and amplitude
material response
camera requirements
state and relationship conditions
effective story range
reference or performance assets required
```

The signature move is not mandatory in every episode. Repetition without function weakens it. A generated accident, unapproved flourish, or target artifact is not a signature and must not enter the character profile automatically.

## 7. Dialogue performance

For each important line, direct:

- objective and subtext;
- breath before, during, or after;
- gaze target;
- posture and hands;
- sentence shape, rhythm, and volume;
- interruption, hesitation, or repair;
- stable visual window;
- listener reaction.

Complex contact, relocation, and camera movement can compete with dialogue. Use a stable hold, source performance, or another audio route.

## 8. Relationship-specific performance

Relationship and context change performance. Record:

- public versus private posture;
- allowed distance and contact;
- who initiates approach;
- eye contact tolerance;
- object-sharing behavior;
- authority and obligation;
- current conflict, repair, trust, or concealment state;
- secrets each participant knows.

Relationship state is distinct from current emotion. Do not canonize one render gesture without approval.

## 9. Environmental performance

Environment changes behavior:

- cold tightens posture and changes breath;
- heat changes clothing, sweat, pace, and distance;
- humidity changes fur, hair, cloth, and fatigue;
- wind changes stance and loose materials;
- wet ground changes foot placement and speed;
- loud machinery changes speaking and attention;
- cultural and social setting changes public mask, clothing, gesture, and contact.

Use approved physiology and locale rules. Environment may propose, but not canonize, adaptation.

## 10. Performance review

- the action has a clear cause;
- performance belongs to the current state and relationship;
- signals use approved anatomy;
- felt, expressed, masked, and physiological states remain distinct;
- selected details perform different jobs;
- expression scale matches shot duration and complexity;
- the selected viewpoint can actually show or hear the signal;
- dialogue has a stable performance window;
- environment and materials respond consistently;
- final behavior forms a usable landing.

## 11. Material and light as performance

Bodies and materials can carry performance without adding a separate gesture.

- a shoulder line loosens gradually after tension;
- the base of a tail moves before the visible sweep;
- one ridge of fur, feather, hair, cloth, or panel lifts in alertness;
- wet material clings and changes silhouette, while drying restores volume;
- a sleeve, collar, mane, wing edge, horn, or casing catches a practical light source;
- a held object shifts pressure before a decision;
- a character blocks light from another subject, reading as protection or threat according to blocking and reaction;
- a silhouette gains facial or surface detail while approaching a light source;
- a flickering practical source reveals guarded performance in intervals;
- stepping out of shadow can mark an approved arc change when used sparingly.

Name light source, movement, and landing. Do not use lighting as a generic emotional label.

## 12. Choosing a performance from persona and intention

Keep the full applicable persona and its source available to the director. Decide what this occasion should convey or withhold, taking account of experience, habitual stance, the other participant, present conditions, and the intended treatment. The same named feeling need not produce the same expression. A held stance, a delayed reply, an exaggerated movement, or no outward response can each be appropriate.

At a material fork, compare concrete realizations and name the reason for the selected one. Do not generate alternatives for every small operation. Retain the source applicability and any intentional departure, its reason, scope, and what remains invariant in `direction`.

Describe the observable construction at the precision this task needs: participating structures from the declared subject, support, orientation, contact, timing, and the relation to the frame or sound. Several coordinated changes may be one intelligible action. A performance can also contain parallel actions and responsive stillness. Do not reduce this to one body part or impose a fixed distance and duration.

Check the chosen tool's actual inputs and returned evidence separately. A description a performer can follow is useful direction, not proof that a generation tool can realize it. An image fixes one instant; two consistent poses do not establish feasible motion between them. Use timed evidence when the unresolved question is speed, response, support transfer, or synchronization.

## 13. Coordinated details and simultaneous actions

Give each selected detail a job in the intended reading. Keep causal or expressive coordination explicit, and separate it from unrelated instructions competing for the same control. The number of details is not a universal limit. Inspect the whole result before deciding whether a gesture, sound, framing change, or held interval obscures or supports another.

A repair may require linked changes in timing, support, and framing. Record that set and the observation it addresses. Do not split a necessary coordinated action merely to satisfy a one-change rule.

## 14. Species and form vocabulary idea bank

This is a menu for character profiles, not a universal rule set. The approved body plan and current state decide what applies.

- **Human and humanlike forms:** jaw setting before a reply; knuckles whitening on a held object; a swallow moving the throat; heat rising at ears or neck; weight shifting between feet; a slow exhale timed with a decision; a sleeve pushed up or collar loosened as visible intent.
- **Long-muzzle canine forms:** tail-tip flick before a wider wag; ears folding forward before a snarl; a slightly open mouth with an eye crease as a smile substitute; nose-tip contact leaving one cool damp point; restrained mouthing that holds a wrist without biting.
- **Broad-muzzle feline forms:** slow blink as affection; head-bump with low purr; tail tip twitching before a lash; pupils rounding when pleased; a rough tongue over the hairline as an intimate family gesture.
- **Avian forms:** ruff or crest height tracking arousal; feathers slicked thin before threat; a quick beak-tip tap; beak-side nuzzle; careful preening of a companion's hair when the relationship supports it; a small hop the character tries to hide.
- **Scaled and draconic forms:** low throat hum felt as chest resonance; wing membranes half-spreading with excitement; scale sheen changing under state or light; slit pupils softening toward round; warm breath across hair or fur as gentle contact.
- **Equine and large herbivore forms:** ears stopping mid-swivel when fully absorbed; hoof stamp as punctuation; skin shivering along flank at light touch; neck height and head angle carrying caution or trust.
- **Robots and machines:** optic aperture changing before head rotation; servo speed slowing at hesitation; panel alignment tightening under authority; a manipulator pausing above contact; emissive intensity shifting with approved state; cooling vents, fans, or casing vibration carrying physiological equivalents.

Choose a signal and strength from the declared persona, form and purpose. Describe observable motion, held relation, or absence of a signal rather than using the species label as an instruction.

## 15. Large form-specific action in short segments

Select the number, amplitude, and overlap of actions from their purpose and the actual operation's supported controls. Preparation and recovery are useful when they make support, contact, or transformation readable; they are not mandatory phases for every depiction.

A discovered limitation belongs to the tested target, operation, form, duration, and evidence. It is not a rule for every short segment. When direct generation cannot carry a required sequence, consider timed references, key poses, separated elements, compositing, or editing. Preserve the necessary expression rather than automatically reducing it to a single small gesture.

### Reusing prepared scene Persona material

Use [Scene Persona material](#scene-persona-material-prepare-once-reuse-within-scope) to distinguish full-source preparation from scoped reuse.
On first preparation or materially changed scope, read the complete applicable Persona, intent, world and
state, and save the exact definitions together with the scene applications. A prepared material document
is not a short trait summary. For ordinary writing, local repair and resumption within that scope, verify
the complete source hashes and use the prepared document; do not require the full-template preparation
read again. Source changes, new topics or participants, knowledge changes and unsupported choices reopen
preparation. Structural validation cannot certify semantic completeness or the truth of a reading claim.

---

<!-- Source: references/atmosphere-quality.md -->

# Weather, Light, Environment, and Image Quality

Atmosphere is a physical system. It changes bodies, materials, sound, visibility, or movement instead of replacing scene content with a mood label.

## 1. Environment as an acting system

Build atmosphere from linked evidence:

```text
far carrier
near carrier
body or object interaction
sound or light consequence
```

A sequence may distribute these carriers across shots, but the environment must remain one coherent state.

## 2. Weather carriers and body response

### Wind

- far: trees, flags, rain, smoke;
- near: paper, hems, hair, fur tips, dust;
- body: narrowed eyes, held collar, braced balance;
- sound: wind around a named structure or opening.

### Rain

- far: rain layers and cloud light;
- middle: roof, rail, umbrella, awning, or window impact;
- near: puddle, sleeve water, runoff, drops, splash;
- body: wet surfaces, changed grip, careful footing, sheltering gesture;
- sound: continuous bed plus a functional impact or change.

### Snow

- far: layered fall and reduced distant contrast;
- near: flakes landing on a visible surface;
- body: footprints, melting, breath when plausible, altered clothing;
- light: environmental fill that preserves local color.

### Heat and humidity

- environment: haze, still air, fan movement, condensation;
- body: sweat, flushing, breath, fatigue, changed pace or distance;
- wardrobe: loosened collar, rolled sleeves, carried outer layer;
- surface: damp hair, clumped fur, or fabric adhesion where exposure supports it.

## 3. Water and wet state

Record:

- source and direction;
- first contact point;
- darkening, clinging, flattening, or transparency pattern;
- drops, runoff, ripples, splash, wake, or condensation source;
- reflection and refraction;
- drying process when time passes.

Use state events or processes when wetness persists. A cut does not restore dry clothing or fur without elapsed time, shelter, drying, replacement, or another approved event.

## 4. Smoke, steam, dust, fog, and suspended material

Record source, density, depth, movement, light interaction, and occlusion.

- steam rises from hot water, food, breath, or a device;
- smoke follows fire, exhaust, incense, or a held object;
- dust follows footsteps, impact, collapse, or airflow;
- fog occupies environment depth;
- spray and particles follow a named source and path.

Keep identity, action, and contact readable. Local atmosphere should not become a global softness filter unless the visual language requires it.

## 5. Light source, landing, and continuity

For each important source, record:

```text
location
color and intensity relationship
direction
landing surfaces
shadow side
movement or flicker
effective story range
```

Name practical sources when possible. When a subject blocks, enters, or leaves light, treat that change as performance and state evidence. Camera changes may reveal different landing surfaces, but source position and scene logic remain coherent.

A hard low source puts a body on screen twice: as the body itself, and as the shadow it throws. The
cast shadow is a second image of the same pose, lying flat, and when the source is behind the subject
that shadow reaches toward the camera and is frequently the more readable of the two. Direct it as a
subject in its own right:

```text
what the shadow falls on
which way it points and how far it reaches
whose pose it repeats, and which parts of that pose survive in it
whether it touches or crosses anything else in frame
```

A backlit figure whose shadow is left unspecified will still cast one, and it will show whatever pose
the surface invents, which can contradict the figure it belongs to. If the shadow is not wanted in
frame, say where it goes instead.

## 6. Color stability in warm scenes

A warm practical source should read as a source, not as an unrestricted global grade. Two techniques help preserve color and material clarity when the approved style calls for balanced warmth.

### Dual reference temperature

Keep one cool or neutral reference visible when a warm source dominates, such as night beyond a window, an off-white wall, pale stone, glass, or neutral metal. The reference lets the viewer read the warmth as light landing from a source. Without it, local illumination can collapse into a uniform warm cast.

Record both the warm source and the neutral or cool reference, including where each remains visible across coverage.

### Specular anchors

Name one or two clean reflective edges, such as a glass rim, metal handle, polished fitting, wet rail, or instrument edge. Use them as local sharpness, exposure, and white-balance references. Keep them subordinate to the subject rather than distributing bright highlights across every surface.

These are optional production techniques, not universal style requirements. Do not add them to a flat, matte, printed, or intentionally degraded visual language when they conflict with the approved grammar.

## 7. Layered environmental density

A lived-in image does not require maximum object count. Organize density by depth and story function.

- **Foreground:** one or two large clean framing shapes, a contact surface, or the nearest active object.
- **Midground:** the active path, characters, props, and clustered story details that explain current use.
- **Background:** simple depth completion, location identity, and a small number of continuity landmarks.

Use traces of life such as one laundry line, smoke source, vegetable row, worn handle, half-used work surface, or weathered path instead of enumerating every possible object. Each detail should support geography, state, culture, action, or atmosphere.

Keep the movement lane and focal hierarchy readable. Background density may increase across a wide establishing shot and decrease across close coverage while preserving the same approved location.

## 8. Viewpoint and atmosphere

Different viewpoints expose different evidence of the same environment:

- external wide: weather direction, ground, practical light placement;
- external close-up: breath, moisture, reflected light, surface response;
- over-the-shoulder: foreground material response and facing-subject reaction;
- first person: visible sleeve or limb, breath, glare, hearing, balance;
- device or fixed observer: mount, shelter, lens, and microphone limits.

A viewpoint change does not reset weather, temperature, humidity, wetness, light, or sound.

## 9. Night scenes

Night remains legible through motivated sources such as moon, lamp, screen, fire, sign, vehicle light, window spill, or reflected street light.

Track source positions, readable surfaces, darkest permitted areas, reflective response, exposure across cuts, and the source that bridges the seam. Avoid uniform darkness, unrestricted blue grading, or unmotivated rim light.

## 10. Cultural, seasonal, and locale context

Approved world and locale rules may affect:

- dress, layers, footwear, and carried objects;
- architecture, practical lights, and materials;
- public distance, gesture, and contact;
- soundscape and language behavior;
- seasonal weather, vegetation, food, and routine.

Do not infer costume or behavior from a broad location label alone. Use approved evidence and character ownership.

## 11. Environment-driven appearance adaptation

Resolve a proposed adaptation from:

```text
environment facts
character physiology and body plan
owned wardrobe and inventory
approved locale and social rules
scene role and action needs
```

Possible changes include rolled sleeves, removed outerwear, gloves, umbrella, fan, wet fur, dusty clothing, or altered footwear. Record whether the proposal is scene-local, temporary, progressive, or persistent. It becomes canon only after approval.

## 12. Image quality follows the approved visual language

Do not append photorealistic quality language to an illustrated project. Preserve the approved line, shape, shading, surface, palette, edge, texture, and motion grammar.

Use clear focal hierarchy, coherent materials, controlled shadow and highlight placement, deliberate color balance, and readable contact. Intentional degradation must name a specific process and remain consistent.

## 13. Atmosphere and quality review

- sources, depth, and carriers are clear;
- weather affects something visible or audible;
- focal subjects and action remain readable;
- state persists correctly across shots;
- light source and landing stay coherent;
- material response is localized;
- viewpoint changes do not reset environment;
- sound beds and tails support continuity;
- cultural adaptation is approved and specific;
- quality follows the project style.

---

<!-- Source: references/dialogue-and-audio.md -->

# Dialogue, Character Grammar, Audio, and Localization

Dialogue, sound, point of audition, music, subtitles, and localization are separate production layers. Preserve the exact line and its character function even when the selected picture-generation pass cannot carry it.

## 1. Dialogue ownership and character grammar

Every recurring character owns a language system in `character-profiles.md`:

- **Thinking grammar:** how the character organizes the world, such as verdicts, trades, commands, denials, questions, rituals, diagnoses, bargains, or gifts.
- **Vocabulary domain:** terms drawn from profession, origin, status, hobby, body plan, community, or worldview.
- **Sentence fingerprint:** habitual length, omissions, order, repairs, particles, interruptions, and rhythm.
- **Speech stage:** what vocabulary, grammar, register, or language ability is currently available at this story time.
- **Voice direction:** pitch range, breath, pace, texture, volume, and stable limits.

A catchphrase is optional. A way of thinking is mandatory.

Use two tests:

1. **Name-masking test:** hide the speaker label. The line should still sound attributable to the intended character.
2. **Function test:** if the line only explains visible room layout, repeats the action, or recites lore the scene already shows, give that job to image, action, or state and remove the line.

Verification exercise: have several characters express the same fact. Their logic, vocabulary, and sentence shape should differ.

## 2. Common synthetic-dialogue tells

Fix these on sight:

- complete explanatory sentences where a fragment, pause, or gesture would be natural;
- dialogue any character could say without changing wording;
- emotion stated directly instead of leaking through choice, avoidance, rhythm, repair, or action;
- customer-service politeness unrelated to the character or social context;
- symmetrical question-and-answer exchanges that feel mechanically balanced;
- stock exclamations and generic acknowledgements;
- translation shaped by another language's syntax rather than the spoken language;
- every visible speaker receiving a line merely because they are present;
- repeated restatement of names, relationships, objectives, or objects already established;
- lines that summarize hidden motives the focal character cannot know;
- identical sentence length and punctuation across the cast;
- dialogue that forces a complex hand action, relocation, and camera move into the same performance window.

A list is diagnostic, not a demand for slang or fragments. A formal or verbose character may use complete sentences when that is part of the approved grammar.

## 3. Conversation and revision rules

- Prefer action answers, counter-questions, partial answers, delayed answers, and sideways answers when character-true.
- A silent reaction is valid and may be stronger than a reply.
- Draft the full intended exchange, then cut at least one third of the words unless the character's established voice is deliberately verbose.
- Keep the exact speech stage. Do not unlock fluency, vocabulary, intimacy, or confidence early.
- Let interruption, hesitation, self-correction, unfinished lines, and breath carry character when appropriate.
- Preserve asymmetry. One character may ask while another acts, refuses, redirects, or remains silent.
- During important dialogue, keep body and camera movement small unless the movement is the line's one dominant action or a source performance carries it.
- Give listeners specific reactions instead of filling every pause with another line.
- Keep public mask, private behavior, current emotion, and long-term relationship distinct.

Cutting words must not erase the line's objective, speaker ownership, social register, or state. Concision is not neutralization.

## 4. Dialogue function

Each important line should perform at least one concrete job:

- pursue or resist an objective;
- change a relationship position;
- reveal or conceal information;
- force a decision;
- redirect action;
- complete a transfer, promise, refusal, warning, diagnosis, or invitation;
- expose a character-specific contradiction;
- create a sound bridge or performance hold;
- plant or pay off a recurring phrase.

Record the function in the director package. The exact line remains in the canonical dialogue language.

## 5. Dialogue density and stable visual window

Place important dialogue during a stable pose, a small linked action, or a designed performance-transfer window.

A useful window provides:

- a settled camera or one simple related movement;
- a readable face, deliberate profile, or intentional offscreen speaker;
- limited competing hand work;
- enough time for line length and breath;
- a chosen audio route;
- a reaction or landing after the line.

Possible routes include:

- same-pass native generation;
- separate speech generation;
- performance or lip-synchronization operation;
- audio post over a stable visual hold;
- offscreen dialogue;
- subtitle or caption track.

Preserve exact line, speaker, language, voice, timing, and performance even when the route changes. If a line competes with difficult movement, move it after landing, shorten it according to character grammar, split the shot, or route it to another pass.

## 6. Compose in the spoken language

Compose each line directly in the canonical spoken language and register. Do not write an English sentence first and mechanically translate it.

Preserve:

- subject omission;
- politeness or status level;
- particles and repair patterns;
- dialect and region;
- rhythm and sentence length;
- culturally natural indirectness;
- character-specific terminology;
- speech-stage limitations.

When a target has limited spoken-language support, keep the canonical line in project state and choose a separate audio, performance, or subtitle route. Do not silently change the character's language.

## 7. Point of audition

Point of audition is independent from camera ownership.

### Objective mix

Balanced scene audio with spatial direction and no privileged internal hearing.

### Character-subjective audition

May include breathing, pulse, ringing, reduced distant sound, altered clarity, or selective emphasis tied to one character's current state.

### Device or remote audio

Follows the declared microphone, mount, compression, obstruction, transmission path, distance, and recording limits.

A viewpoint cut does not automatically change the mix. Record the cause and transition when audition changes.

## 8. Functional sound design

Build the scene mix from:

```text
ambient bed
action and contact sounds
turn or reveal effect
dialogue and breath
music function
bridge or tail
```

Sound needs a source and production function. It may:

- cause an action;
- reveal space;
- carry a cut;
- confirm contact or material response;
- support performance;
- mask a seam;
- preserve continuity across clips.

Examples:

- rain on an awning causes a character to glance upward;
- pendant metal clicks against the workbench during a handoff;
- machinery masks a quiet line until the motor stops;
- a continuous room bed bridges an external shot to a first-person insert;
- a fixed camera microphone loses clarity when a door closes between it and the speaker.

Use the scoped wording rules in [`scoped-lexicon.md`](#scoped-lexicon-visual-prose-and-constraint-wording). Sound terms such as `muffled` or `faint` belong to a sound path, not to the whole visual grade.

## 9. Music

Record instrumentation, pulse, register, motif, authority, and edit function rather than relying only on a song title.

Music can:

- establish series, character, relationship, or location identity;
- build pressure;
- step aside for a functional turn effect;
- return after a decision;
- bridge scenes;
- track a chapter or long character arc;
- distinguish narrator-present from depicted-past time.

Plan music across the edited sequence when per-clip generation would restart it. Generated music is candidate material until accepted and mixed.

For narration or retrospective structure:

- keep only low sustained texture under dense narration and let melody answer in pauses;
- play the heaviest line dry, with no music, when that absence carries the intended weight;
- use an in-world metronome, such as knitting needles, sword practice, machinery, footsteps, or a fire, when a diegetic sound can keep time more naturally than a drum part;
- **Cross-episode motif arc:** let a recurring motif travel across episodes as complete, fragmented, off-key, varied, withheld, and finally complete when that progression tracks approved character or relationship state.

Do not force these techniques into every narration scene. Record the concrete cue, source, and edit point.

## 10. Subtitles and captions

Subtitles default to off. When requested, each track declares:

- language;
- exact text;
- delivery route: sidecar, metadata, burned-in, or another actual route;
- timing after picture is locked;
- translation style;
- speaker identification when needed;
- line breaks and reading constraints;
- authoritative source text.

Keep sidecar and metadata subtitles outside visual prompts. Burned-in text requires explicit intent and current target evidence.

## 11. Localization

Conversation language, director-package language, target-submitted language, each character's dialogue language, and subtitle languages are independent.

Translate from canonical dialogue, not from another translation. Preserve:

- character grammar;
- social register;
- names and glossary;
- intentional ambiguity;
- wordplay adaptation;
- speech stage;
- subtitle length and reading constraints.

Record deliberate adaptation, honorific loss, register change, or replacement wordplay instead of claiming literal equivalence.

## 12. Beat-template fatigue

Keep causal structure while rotating surface form. Review the previous two episodes or comparable scenes before finalizing a recurring pattern.

Possible openings:

- sound first;
- visible hand already mid-task;
- peripheral entrance;
- awakened by weight or contact;
- light opening through a gap;
- a held object already in dispute;
- continued action from the prior scene.

Possible reactions:

- listener close-up;
- held prop or interrupted task;
- posture change;
- offscreen line while watching the listener;
- silent refusal;
- delayed answer after contact or state settles.

Possible endings:

- object rather than face;
- character back or departing silhouette;
- dying sound;
- unfinished line;
- one body part or hand state;
- continuing action bridge;
- quiet relationship consequence;
- purposeful unresolved image.

Do not rotate forms mechanically. Change them when repeated sentence shape, coverage pattern, dialogue exchange, or landing would make adjacent episodes feel interchangeable.

## 13. Audio continuity and finishing

Across clips and shots, track:

- ambient bed and room-tone source;
- dialogue tail;
- effect decay;
- music phrase;
- point of audition;
- spatial direction;
- intended J-cut or L-cut;
- generated versus post-produced authority;
- final loudness target and destination.

Generated audio is candidate material. Review each track separately. Finish accepted audio before extracting an endpoint when sound affects the next cut.

A surface that produces audio alongside picture produces it per clip, which means it restarts at every
clip boundary and arrives at a different level in every take. That restart lands exactly on the seam
the edit is trying to hide, so treat generated audio as a source of foley and a scratch guide, never
as the programme's ambience. Measure the takes before assuming otherwise: a spread of more than a few
units of loudness across the clips of one scene is the level jump the audience will hear at every cut.

Lay the ambience instead as one unbroken region spanning every clip of the sequence, never one region
per clip and never re-triggered at a cut. The check is direct: mute the bed and replay two adjacent
shots. Muted, the cut should read as two different places; with the bed, as one place from two angles.
The bed buys nothing for state or identity errors, only for the seam.

Two practical constraints follow.

First, the bed has to come from somewhere. A video surface that generates sound tends to generate
events rather than sustained tone, so its output usually cannot be looped into a bed at all; check by
measuring the flattest few seconds available and looking at the spread. When there is no library, a
sound-design model asked for an explicitly unchanging environment is the cheapest source, and a
seamless-loop option where the surface offers one.

Second, do not run a dynamic loudness normaliser over the finished mix. It rides the gain, lifting the
quiet stretches and ducking the ones carrying foley, which reintroduces movement at the very cuts the
bed was laid to cover. Measure the programme once and apply a fixed gain.

## 14. Dialogue and audio completion check

- every important line has exact text, speaker, language, voice, and route;
- name-masking and function tests pass;
- synthetic-dialogue tells have been reviewed;
- current speech stage is preserved;
- dialogue length fits a stable visual window or has an alternate route;
- silence is used when stronger than another line;
- the previous two episodes were checked for repeated surface form when relevant;
- camera and audition relationships are deliberate;
- ambient bed and key effects have sources;
- music has a scene or sequence function;
- narration and motif techniques are intentional rather than automatic;
- subtitle policy is explicit;
- localization preserves character grammar and speech stage;
- audio bridges and tails support continuity;
- generated and post-produced authority is recorded;
- no ungenerated audio result is claimed.

---

<!-- Source: references/post-production.md -->

# Post-Production, Joins, Alternate Paths, and Finishing

Finishing happens before continuity extraction. Accept the picture, repair and conform it, finish joins, grade and mix, then extract the terminal frame and audio tail that seed continuation.

## 1. Boundary types and planning record

Every boundary is one of:

- **continuity join:** story time continues and the boundary should read as one physical progression;
- **scene transition:** time, place, focalization, viewpoint source, or dramatic unit changes deliberately.

Plan the boundary before generation:

```text
boundary ID
boundary type
outgoing shot and accepted endpoint requirement
incoming shot and required opening
bridge type
match point
action phase
state, wardrobe, and prop continuity
picture finishing route
audio bridge
fallback
```

A seam is part of shot design, not a problem discovered only after generation.

## 2. What separately generated clips do not promise

Do not assume that a target replays a supplied anchor frame exactly. Record whether the exact surface reuses pixels, re-synthesizes the boundary, or leaves the behavior unknown. Independent generations commonly introduce several different discontinuities:

- small near-constant shifts in framing, scale, crop, rotation, or lens relation;
- repainted fur, skin, cloth, rain, grain, texture, and local detail;
- changed state or prop geometry even when the broad pose matches;
- restarted ambience, dialogue, effects, or music;
- altered exposure, white balance, saturation, or contrast.

Pixel continuity across independent generations is an editing deliverable. Plan the boundary pose, camera state, action phase, and match point during shot and clip packing. Register against what the viewer last saw in the delivered outgoing clip, not against a planned anchor that the generator may have reinterpreted.

Match exposure, white balance, and saturation before using a dissolve, mask, or texture layer. A geometry match can still expose a cut through grade or surface repaint. A short crossfade may hide a near-still texture change but can ghost fast motion.

## 3. Continuity joins

Preferred order:

1. execute the designed action, eyeline, object, occlusion, sound, or light bridge;
2. use a registered cut when both boundaries nearly match;
3. use a masked cut with a scene-motivated transient;
4. use a short dissolve or optical-flow morph only when motion and style support it;
5. unify grade, texture, and audio bed across the join.

Useful join methods include:

- cut on action;
- eyeline cut;
- object match;
- occlusion cut;
- blink bridge when camera ownership permits it;
- light bridge;
- registered hard cut;
- masked cut;
- short dissolve for elapsed time;
- sound bridge;
- generated transition backed by a real operation.

A crossfade can hide texture repaint in a near-still shot but may ghost fast motion. Inspect the result rather than assuming it works.

## 4. Scene transitions

Use transition grammar intentionally:

- hard cut for direct contrast or continued energy;
- dissolve for elapsed time or gradual relationship;
- fade for chapter boundary, sleep, loss of consciousness, or deliberate closure;
- match cut on shape, action, color, object, or sound;
- sound bridge to connect place or time;
- blink or occlusion when consistent with camera ownership;
- generated transition only with a supported operation and inspected boundaries.

A transition used only to hide a failed continuity join remains a repair, not a story transition.

## 5. Generated transition record

A generated transition is a distinct operation. Record:

```text
bridge type: generated transition
outgoing operand
outgoing endpoint anchor
incoming operand
incoming endpoint anchor
generated bridge output
operation
story duration
generated duration
operation-card or run ID
exact source-to-control mapping
primary and auxiliary field contents
acceptance criteria
fallback edit
```

The operation must accept the required source operands or endpoint controls together. Both boundaries must be inspected and physically compatible. Otherwise preserve the intended bridge in the director package and use a conventional edit, intermediate bridge asset, or redesigned shot.

## 6. Registration and conform

- conform every boundary to delivery dimensions before submission and editing;
- register to delivered frames, not planned anchors;
- compare position, scale, rotation, crop, lens relation, state, and content;
- use a constant transform for constant drift;
- animate correction only when drift changes;
- correct on the side with available framing room;
- prefer safe crop-in over invented borders;
- record every transform.

Independent generations often repaint fur, cloth, texture, grain, and noise even when geometry matches. Match grade first, then use a shared subtle texture layer only when the approved visual language and delivery require it.

## 7. Masked-event calibration

A flash, shadow, steam burst, passing object, screen flicker, spray, or light sweep used to mask a cut must follow the scene's own visual grammar.

Record:

- source;
- location;
- color;
- attack and decay;
- maximum intensity;
- relation to existing events;
- frames covered;
- fallback.

Light the source first and keep spill subordinate. Do not create a mask stronger than the storm, lamp, screen, steam, or passing object it claims to be.

## 8. Picture repair and alternate paths

Possible repairs include:

- crop and reframe;
- stabilization;
- retime;
- freeze or micro-extension;
- identity or state correction;
- contact and prop repair;
- background cleanup;
- paint-out;
- color and exposure match;
- compositing;
- typography and subtitle insertion.

Record source, method, parameters, result, limitations, and rejoin point. Any repair that changes final pixels occurs before terminal-frame extraction.

When a requirement was not completed in picture generation, preserve it in an alternate-path plan:

| Requirement | Why this pass did not carry it | Next operation or post path | Required source and output | Acceptance | Rejoin point |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Unsupported does not mean deleted.

## 9. Audio finishing

- maintain room tone or environment bed across ordinary cuts;
- use J-cuts and L-cuts intentionally;
- preserve dialogue, effects, ambience, and music as separate stems when precision matters;
- remove restarts and discontinuities;
- align contact sounds with picture;
- preserve point-of-audition transitions;
- record loudness target and true-peak limit for the actual destination;
- measure the final master.

Common starting points include approximately -14 LUFS integrated for many streaming deliveries, -16 LUFS for some spoken-audio or podcast norms, and -23 LUFS under EBU R128 broadcast practice, with true peak commonly kept at or below -1 dBTP. These are examples, not suite defaults. Verify the actual destination specification before mastering and record the selected target.

Generated audio is candidate material, not automatic authority. Choose, replace, or rebuild it according to the approved plan.

The ambient bed is the mortar between clips. A simultaneously restarting picture and audio boundary usually exposes the cut even when geometry matches.

## 10. Viewpoint-switch finishing

A viewpoint switch may require:

- registered object or eyeline match;
- audio-perspective transition;
- camera-owner breath or device sound entering or leaving;
- external reaction timing;
- state-visibility adjustment;
- motion blur or occlusion at the bridge;
- restoration of shared geography after a subjective insert.

Review whether the switch changes only camera, or also focalization, audition, knowledge, and visible state. Preserve prop ownership, action phase, relationship distance, light, and accepted geometry.

## 11. Terminal-frame and audio-tail extraction

Extract continuation evidence only from the accepted finished master.

Record:

- exact file and content hash;
- timecode or frame number;
- dimensions and aspect;
- camera profile, position, roll, lens relation, and facing;
- visible subjects and state;
- prop ownership and orientation;
- contact and support;
- light and atmosphere;
- dialogue, music, effect, and ambience tail;
- repair and conform history.

The next shot begins from this evidence or from an explicitly created, registered, and inspected corrective derivative.

## 12. Machine-first verification

Before human viewing, verify what tools can measure:

- container integrity;
- duration and frame rate;
- resolution and aspect;
- track presence;
- boundary scale, position, rotation, and content agreement;
- black frames or frozen sections;
- audio levels and clipping;
- subtitle or metadata tracks;
- file hashes;
- expected shot, state, and asset records.

Human review then focuses on motion, acting, audio content, seam belonging, taste, and acceptance. A geometry or level defect that measurement could have found first is a process failure, not normal viewing work.

## 13. Finishing record and completion check

Preserve:

```text
source clips and assets
edit timeline
boundary decisions
registration transforms
masked events
picture repairs
alternate paths and rejoin points
grade and texture
stems and mix
typography and subtitles
measured delivery properties
accepted master
extracted terminal frame and audio tail
```

Completion requires:

- every boundary has a declared type, treatment, and fallback;
- picture conforms to delivery dimensions;
- state, identity, wardrobe, props, contact, and action match across joins;
- viewpoint and audition changes are deliberate;
- audio bed and perspective are continuous or intentionally changed;
- repairs and alternate paths are recorded and reproducible;
- final master passes machine checks;
- terminal evidence comes from the finished master;
- asset registry and production state point to accepted outputs.

---

<!-- Source: references/visual-contracts.md -->

# Visual contracts, and the shot-request that binds them

Visual design specifies what a subject is; shot direction specifies how it appears at a particular story point. Develop the design from the brief, persona and world, then choose the view, performance and references that express the scene's purpose. Use contracts to retain the approved decisions and expose unresolved details.

A content hash identifies a record. Approval establishes its use; media inspection establishes what a particular image or recording supports. Keep those conclusions separate.

## 1. Which authority settles what

Use the narrowest relevant authority for each decision. The [responsibility layers](#21-responsibility-layers) distinguish intent, facts, direction, execution, observation and acceptance; a public data representation does not change a record's role:

| Evidence | What it can settle |
|---|---|
| Approved narrative, persona, and world design | Story purpose, identity, character intent, knowledge, and the author's portrayal choices |
| Species Morphology Profile | The declared body-plan possibility space, capabilities, and meaningful absences |
| Individual Morphology and Character Identity Contracts | This subject's declared realization and enduring appearance |
| Era, Form, and Appearance Variant Contracts | Approved changes with explicit applicability and lineage |
| Approved events and processes | Variable state at the requested story point |
| Scene context, camera, and projection | What this shot can show and which features need visible support |
| Adopted media and observed endpoints | What a particular file actually proves, in its recorded scope |
| Target observations and exact submissions | What was bound, sent, returned, and measured for this operation |

An event cannot rewrite stable anatomy through an undeclared state path. A generated image cannot approve a new body feature, a relationship, or a story event. A declaration of intended appearance cannot prove that media depicts it.

## 2. Visual design and the morphology stack

For a new recurring subject, develop the persona and world context with the author, record stable visual anchors in `character-profiles.md`, and use the bundled schemas and templates when a machine-readable morphology or state binding is needed. Draft missing fields explicitly; do not fill them from a species label or from assumed human anatomy. An unresolved field stays unresolved until the needed decision or evidence exists.

Draft the applicable Species, Individual, Identity, Era, Form and Appearance documents. After substantive review, validate and seal each artifact with `scripts/state_protocol.py finalize INPUT --out OUTPUT`, then validate it. Sealing identifies the bytes; approval is still a separate author decision. Keep superseded accepted records for their historical ranges.

```text
species morphology profile
+ individual morphology contract
+ character identity contract
+ applicable era, form, and appearance contracts
+ approved state at a story point
+ scene, pose, clothing, visibility and camera
= shot-resolved morphology and identity
```

A species profile owns possibilities, not a compulsory instance. An individual contract owns stable feature IDs, measurements, markings, asymmetries, expression carriers, mutable paths and unknowns. A projection owns only the selected visible result. A hidden feature remains in the body plan.

Before using a record, establish its approval status, supporting evidence and applicable story range. For prose and comics, apply the visual contracts needed by the intended panels or passages; do not impose shot geometry on prose.

## 3. Per-shot request

A `shot-request` records the image evidence required for a shot. It binds the applicable design, state, scene and camera to visible obligations and a deliverable. Sending a generation request requires the separate submission approval.

It carries:

```text
species_profile_sha256_by_character
individual_morphology_sha256_by_character
identity_contract_sha256_by_character
state_snapshot_sha256_by_character
visible_morphology_feature_refs_by_character
scene_context_sha256
camera_spec_sha256
shot_projection_sha256
scene_plot_sha256 and narrative_sha256 when linked
viewpoint_profile_id
visible_identity_obligations and visible_state_obligations
selected_reference_candidates
deliverable and required_output (including aspect_ratio when applicable)
```

Validate the exact request and its referenced files with `scripts/shot_request.py`. Use `--require-complete` when all named bindings must be checked before handoff. A request inspected alone reports unverified bindings, not a complete approval. Empty character maps are valid for a character-free view; never invent a protagonist to fill them.

### The narrative contract

[The narrative protocol](../protocols/narrative/README.md) describes what the series is about. The author controls the narrative; the agent can propose and edit it under that direction. `scripts/narrative.py`, persona records and `scripts/scene_plot.py` read the production's narrative and scene evidence. Screen scenes are realized as shots, comics as pages and panels, and prose as passages. Derive each shot's purpose and permitted disclosure from its approved scene plot.

### Where the shot came from

Within a narrative project, a scene plot names its narrative and the chapter it realizes. When a shot request carries `scene_plot_sha256`, it records the approved plot content; `narrative_sha256` records the narrative underlying that plot. Both links travel together. A request not linked to a narrative may omit these hashes. A narrative-linked submission requires both. Report missing provenance as unmeasured rather than inventing it.

Camera `knowledge_scope` describes the information reach of one shot. Scene-plot `focalization` describes whose knowledge limits the telling. Neither can silently substitute for the other.

## 4. Camera-dependent morphology and reference selection

Changing camera does not change stable identity; it changes the evidence the frame needs.

- A front close-up needs the visible facial or sensory features and expression carriers.
- A profile needs visible projection, attachment and depth, not unsupported front-view inference.
- A rear shot needs rear markings, attachment sites, clothing and harness paths.
- Full-body action needs declared counts, proportions, contacts and appendage clearance.
- First-person direction concerns the environment and declared visible limbs or tools; it does not require an unseen owner's face.
- Contact needs ownership and contact surfaces for each participating structure, tool, garment and support.

Select adopted references that visibly support those requirements from useful views. A portrait does not prove a hidden attachment. When a selected reference bundle has identity and performance panels, assign their roles separately and record their actual target controls. A performance pose does not redefine identity. If only text establishes a feature, retain the text anchor without claiming visual proof. The author decides whether to create missing reference media under the submission rules.

## 5. Candidate and adoption separation

A Candidate Manifest records a particular generation or import result. Once sealed, it is immutable evidence of that candidate set. Do not edit it to record approval. A separate Adoption Receipt records the actual selection, scope, registry updates, effective story range and supersession.

For morphology-bearing assets, record the contracts and view-specific features the adopted file supports, the unsupported regions or states, its applicable form and story range, and any derivation or accepted repair. The fact that a file passed structure or hash validation does not approve its content. Importing an envelope also does not create an adoption receipt.

A text fact can be canon without an image. It becomes a visual lock only when an adopted carrier proves the relevant appearance. Planned end states and observed endpoint geometry remain distinct.

## 6. State, form, and offscreen change

Resolve approved events and processes in story order, not in the order shots are shown. Temporary state, interruptions, expiry and editorial supersession have explicit rules in the public state contract. Ordinary changes affect declared mutable paths; permanent anatomy or topology changes need approved form or identity lineage.

For a flashback, select the era, form and state valid at that point. For a continuation, inspect the accepted terminal media for the seam while retaining the correct identity and story-state authorities. Use the observed terminal frame to verify the seam; the projected endpoint remains the intended result.

## 7. Drift routing

When a review is requested, identify which authority needs attention. A wrong species declaration goes back to species design; an individual discrepancy goes back to that individual contract; a wrong state goes back to events, processes or resolution. A crop or occlusion does not change anatomy. A failed performance, contact or composition is a shot realization issue. A repeatedly lost feature may justify a target-scoped tactic.

Preserve the candidate and the observation. Present returned work before reviewing it, and do not trigger another paid run from an unrequested self-review. A repair or new generation requires the user's applicable authorization. Deliberate portrayal variation must be distinguished from accidental drift using the approved narrative and character intent.

## 8. Public artifact delivery

When the request includes artifact exchange, select the artifact type, required features and exact referenced content. Use `scripts/protocol_exchange.py` to inspect or export the artifact with its schema contract, and to verify a received bundle. `protocols/contract-manifest.json` lists the public types and `config/protocol-layout.json` resolves their schemas.

For profile-aware exchange, `scripts/build_interchange_envelope.py` writes `artifact.json`, `declaration.json` and `envelope.json`. Validation checks the profile, identifiers, required features and content commitments. Preserve unknown optional features as uninterpreted evidence; stop if a required feature cannot be interpreted.

Record source provenance, approval status and effective range before adopting supplied evidence. Verification is not media inspection, adoption or submission permission. See [Interchange](../protocols/interchange/README.md) for the commands and file contract.

## 9. Re-anchor ladder

Before a shot is realized, reconcile:

1. Approved narrative, portrayal intent and current persona.
2. Applicable identity, morphology and variation contracts.
3. Approved state at the selected story point.
4. Adopted identity and performance carriers with their visible support and limits.
5. Shot geometry, crop, contact, blocking and knowledge scope.
6. Accepted seam media for continuity geometry, never as a replacement for identity.
7. Exact text anchors for details not proved by the selected view.

This is a reconciliation order, not permission to hide a conflict. Stop at the earliest unresolved dependency needed by the operation. Do not average identities or promote an accidental feature.

## 10. Reference activation obligations

When a `visual-contract-package` is selected for an operation, use `scripts/reference_activation_gate.py` before submission.

**The prepared reference set is the reference truth.** Bind the selected set by hash. Do not add or silently drop references during activation. Rebuild a newly reviewed selection when the set genuinely changes.

**Authority is record-scoped.** A reference can settle only its declared dimensions. Identity guidance does not settle shot geometry; text-only authority is not visible evidence. Do not reopen a locked dimension through an undocumented override.

**Activation is reconciled with state.** Observe effective story ranges, supersession and the supported or occluded state. Carry unproved assumptions into the requested review rather than silently settling them.

Unprovided evidence is unmeasured. Check the applicable narrative, submission, target and media-evidence requirements before proceeding.

---

<!-- Source: references/morphology-and-species-contracts.md -->

# Morphology and species contracts

## 1. Purpose

This reference defines how a visual contract records species anatomy, individual anatomy, fictional body plans, expression channels, and shot-visible morphology without reducing a subject to a species name or a loose list of tags.

The system has three canonical layers:

1. `species-morphology-profile`: the valid anatomical and expressive possibility space for a species, lineage, chassis family, or original creature class.
2. `individual-morphology-contract`: the exact realization for one recurring character or one identifiable animal, creature, hybrid, robot, or android.
3. `resolved-morphology`: the shot-specific visible result after current form, state, crop, pose, clothing, occlusion, damage, and expression are applied.

A species noun is not a complete morphology contract. A familiar noun may help retrieval, but every production-critical count, attachment, shape, dimension, surface, motion range, and expression carrier must be declared or explicitly left unresolved.

## 2. Ownership and precedence

Use the following precedence order:

1. Explicit user statement.
2. Approved species or lineage profile.
3. Approved individual morphology contract.
4. Approved current state and form events.
5. Shot-specific resolved morphology.
6. Retrieval heuristics and model priors.
